r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two positive integers n and k.
An integer x is called k-palindromic if:

x is a palindrome.
x is divisible by k.

An integer is called good if its digits can be rearranged to form a k-palindromic integer. For example, for k = 2, 2020 can be rearranged to form the k-palindromic integer 2002, whereas 1010 cannot be rearranged to form a k-palindromic integer.
Return the count of good integers containing n digits.
Note that any integer must not have leading zeros, neither before nor after rearrangement. For example, 1010 cannot be rearranged to form 101.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 3, k = 5
Output: 27
Explanation:
Some of the good integers are:

551 because it can be rearranged to form 515.
525 because it is already k-palindromic.


Example 2:

Input: n = 1, k = 4
Output: 2
Explanation:
The two good integers are 4 and 8.

Example 3:

Input: n = 5, k = 6
Output: 2468

 
Constraints:

1 <= n <= 10
1 <= k <= 9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, implement countGoodIntegers.

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        from math import factorial
        fact = [1]*(n+1)
        for i in range(1,n+1): fact[i]=fact[i-1]*i

        def count_permutations(cnt):
            total = fact[n]
            den = 1
            for c in cnt:
                den *= fact[c]
            total //= den
            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad = fact[n-1]
                den2 = fact[cnt[0]-1]
                for d in range(1,10):
                    den2 *= fact[cnt[d]]
                total -= bad // den2
            return total

        def can_form(cnt):
            # palindrome possible?
            if n % 2 == 0:
                for c in cnt:
                    if c % 2: return False
                half_counts = [c//2 for c in cnt]
            else:
                odd_digits = [d for d,c in enumerate(cnt) if c % 2 == 1]
                if len(odd_digits) != 1:
                    return False
                center = odd_digits[0]
                half_counts = [0]*10
                for d in range(10):
                    if d == center:
                        half_counts[d] = cnt[d]//2 + 1
                    else:
                        half_counts[d] = cnt[d]//2
            # DFS
            m = (n+1)//2
            # maybe if half_counts[0] == m? all half digits zero => leading zero invalid; but if cnt has nonzero maybe? We'll handle by DFS first digit.
            # Build list of digits with remaining counts? Use counts array.
            half_counts = list(half_counts)
            # Precompute? We'll DFS over positions.
            # At each position choose digit d from 0..9 with remaining>0.
            # At pos0, d !=0.
            # At leaf compute mod.
            # Could early stop if found.
            def dfs(pos, half):
                if pos == m:
                    # compute palindrome mod
                    mod = 0
                    if n % 2 == 0:
                        for d in half:
                            mod = (mod*10 + d) % k
                        for d in reversed(half):
                            mod = (mod*10 + d) % k
                    else:
                        for d in half:
                            mod = (mod*10 + d) % k
                        # reversed(half[:-1])
                        for d in reversed(half[:-1]):
                            mod = (mod*10 + d) % k
                    return mod == 0
                start = 1 if pos == 0 else 0
                for d in range(start, 10):
                    if half_counts[d] > 0:
                        half_counts[d] -= 1
                        half.append(d)
                        if dfs(pos+1, half):
                            # restore? Need restore before returning? We can return True after restoring? We need restore to maintain state for backtracking. If return inside, need restore first.
                            half.pop()
                            half_counts[d] += 1
                            return True
                        half.pop()
                        half_counts[d] += 1
                return False
            return dfs(0, [])

        ans=0
        cnt=[0]*10
        def enumerate_digits(pos, remaining):
            nonlocal ans
            if pos == 9:
                cnt[9] = remaining
                # process
                if cnt[0] < n:  # at least one nonzero
                    if can_form(cnt):
                        ans += count_permutations(cnt)
                cnt[9] = 0
                return
            for c in range(remaining+1):
                cnt[pos] = c
                enumerate_digits(pos+1, remaining-c)
            cnt[pos] = 0

        enumerate_digits(0,n)
        return ans

Pseudo can_form:
def can_form(cnt):
    if n % 2 == 0:
        if any(c % 2 for c in cnt): return False
        center = None
        left_counts = [c//2 for c in cnt]
    else:
        odd = [d for d,c in enumerate(cnt) if c%2==1]
        if len(odd) != 1: return False
        center = odd[0]
        left_counts = [c//2 for c in cnt]
    p = n // 2
    # If center is not None and center==0? okay.
    # DFS over left positions
    def dfs(pos, left):
        if pos == p:
            # left[0] must be nonzero. But if p==0, no left; leading digit is center.
            if p > 0 and left[0] == 0:
                return False
            mod = 0
            for d in left:
                mod = (mod*10 + d) % k
            if center is not None:
                mod = (mod*10 + center) % k
            for d in reversed(left):
                mod = (mod*10 + d) % k
            return mod == 0
        start = 1 if pos == 0 else 0
        for d in range(start, 10):
            if left_counts[d] > 0:
                left_counts[d] -= 1
                left.append(d)
                if dfs(pos+1, left):
                    left.pop()
                    left_counts[d] += 1
                    return True
                left.pop()
                left_counts[d] += 1
        return False
    return dfs(0, [])

Approach B:
- Generate all palindromic n-digit numbers (no leading zero) by iterating left half:
   if n even: m=n//2, palindrome = left + reversed(left)
   if n odd: m=n//2+1, palindrome = left + reversed(left[:-1]) (left includes center)
   left range from 10^{m-1} to 10^m -1? For n=1, m=1, left 1..9? Actually no leading zero, left first digit nonzero. For n=1, left [d], palindrome [d].
- For each palindrome, compute mod k; if divisible, compute digit counts tuple cnt[0..9], add to set good_multisets.
- Then for each multiset in good_multisets, count n-digit permutations with that multiset (no leading zero) and sum.
This counts exactly multisets that can form at least one k-palindromic n-digit integer. It avoids checking palindrome possible and center logic. Number of palindromes <= 9*10^{m-1}; for n=10, m=5? Wait n even m=5, left length5, first digit nonzero => 9*10^4=90000. For n=9, m=5, also 90000. So at most 90000. Very manageable. This might be simpler and less bug-prone. Let's compare approach B with approach A in terms of code length. Approach B code:

class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        fact = [1]*(n+1)
        for i in range(1,n+1): fact[i]=fact[i-1]*i

        good = set()
        half_len = (n + 1) // 2
        start = 10 ** (half_len - 1)
        end = 10 ** half_len
        for half in range(start, end):
            s = str(half)
            if n % 2 == 0:
                pal = s + s[::-1]
            else:
                if n == 1:
                    pal = s
                else:
                    pal = s + s[-2::-1]
            if int(pal) % k == 0:
                cnt = [0]*10
                for ch in pal:
                    cnt[int(ch)] += 1
                good.add(tuple(cnt))
        ans = 0
        for cnt in good:
            total = fact[n]
            for c in cnt:
                total //= fact[c]? Wait need divide by product, cannot sequentially integer divide? Since fact[n] divisible by product, sequential integer division is okay? If we do total //= fact[c] for each c, because product divides fact[n], and fact[n]/(fact[a]*fact[b]) = (fact[n]/fact[a])/fact[b], both integer? Since fact[n]/fact[a] integer and then divisible by fact[b]? Yes because multinomial coefficient integer. Sequential floor division should yield exact integer. But to be safe, compute den = product then total //= den. Use den.
            den = 1
            for c in cnt: den *= fact[c]
            total = fact[n] // den
            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad_den = fact[cnt[0]-1]
                for d in range(1,10):
                    bad_den *= fact[cnt[d]]
                bad = fact[n-1] // bad_den
                total -= bad
            ans += total
        return ans

Need handle n=1: half_len=1, start=1, end=10. For n odd, n==1 pal=s. Good.

Potential issue: For n odd and half_len>1, pal = s + s[-2::-1]. Let's verify for half_len=2, s='ab', s[-2::-1] = 'a'? In Python, 'ab'[-2::-1] -> start index -2 = 0, step -1 -> 'a'. pal='aba'. Good. For half_len=3, s='abc', s[-2::-1] -> start index -2 = 1 'b', step -1 -> 'ba'. pal='abcba'. Good. For half_len=5, s='abcde', s[-2::-1] -> 'dcba'? Wait start index -2 = 3 'd', step -1 -> 'dcba'. pal='abcdedcba'? But palindrome for n=9 half_len=5 should s + s[-2::-1] = a b c d e d c b a, yes. Good.

For n even, pal=s+s[::-1]. For half_len=5, s='abcde', pal='abcdeedcba' length10. Good.

Let's enumerate possible multisets and conditions.

A multiset of 5 digits that is palindrome-capable has exactly one odd count. Let center digit be the odd-count digit? Wait in a palindrome, the digit with odd count is the center digit, but if center digit appears in paired positions, its total count is odd (1+2t). The digit with odd count is indeed the digit at center. So center c is the unique odd-count digit. The other digits have even counts. Thus for n=5, multiset counts: center c has count 1,3,or5; other digits have counts 2 or4. So patterns:
- c count1: two other digits each count2 (could same? If same, count4, not two digits; so either two distinct other digits count2, or one other digit count4).
- c count3: one other digit count2.
- c count5: no other digits.

Now, for a multiset to be good, there must exist a palindrome x y c y x with x even nonzero and c ≡ x+y mod3, where x,y are the two paired-position digits (L). L is a multiset of size2 from digits (not necessarily distinct), and center is c. The multiset counts are determined by L and c:
- If L = [x,y] with x≠y: counts x:2, y:2, c:1. Center c count1. x must be even nonzero (first digit). Condition c ≡ x+y mod3.
- If L = [x,x]: counts x:4, c:1. Center c count1. Condition c ≡ 2x mod3. x even nonzero.
- If L = [c,y] with y≠c: counts c:3, y:2. Center c count3. Condition c ≡ c+y mod3 => y ≡0 mod3. Also the first digit of palindrome can be c or y? Wait L = [c,y] means the two paired positions are c and y. The palindrome first digit is L[0], which could be c or y depending on order. For the palindrome to be valid, the first digit must be even nonzero. If L[0]=c, c must be even nonzero. If L[0]=y, y must be even nonzero. But our enumeration with x as first digit handles both orders: if L=[c,y] and first digit x=c, then y is second; condition c ≡ c+y mod3 => y≡0. If first digit x=y, then L=[y,c], condition c ≡ y+c mod3 => y≡0. So in either order, y≡0 mod3, and the first digit (whether c or y) must be even nonzero. Thus for pattern c:3,y:2, we require y≡0 mod3 and at least one of c,y is even nonzero (the first digit). But if first digit is y, y must be even nonzero; if y is 0, cannot be first, so c must be even nonzero. If y is 3 or9 (odd), cannot be first, so c must be even nonzero. If y=6, y can be first or c can be first; need at least one even nonzero. Since c is center digit, could be any digit? Center can be 0? If c=0, then first digit cannot be c, so y must be even nonzero (6). So pattern c:3,y:2 with y≡0 mod3 and (c even nonzero or y even nonzero). But wait if c=0 and y=6, palindrome 6 0 6 0 6? L=[6,0]? first x=6, center c=0, palindrome 6 0 6 0 6? Actually L=[x,y]=[6,0], center c=0, palindrome x y c y x = 6 0 0 0 6? Wait if center c=0 and L=[6,0], palindrome digits: x=6, y=0, center=0, y=0, x=6 => 60006. Counts: 6:2, 0:3. Center digit 0 count3. Condition c ≡ x+y mod3: 0 ≡6+0=6≡0 yes. So pattern c=0 count3, y=6 count2. Good. First digit x=6 even nonzero. So yes.
- If L = [c,c]: counts c:5. Condition c ≡ 2c mod3 => c≡0 mod3. First digit c must be even nonzero. So c=6 only.

So we can count unique multisets by patterns and conditions. Let's do that to verify 2468.

Let's write final code cleanly.

Potential edge cases:
- n=1: half_len=1, start=1, end=10. For odd, pal = s if n==1 else s + s[-2::-1]. int(pal)%k. Count permutations: fact[1]=1. For cnt {4}, total=1, cnt0=0, ans1. For cnt {8}, ans2. Good.
- k=9, n=1: digits divisible by9: 9 only. Our enumeration half 1..9, int%9==0 ->9. ans1. Good.
- n=10,k=9: Palindromes divisible by9 if digit sum divisible by9. Count multisets. Fine.

Let's think about if using int(pal) for n=10 and pal maybe '1000000001'? Actually half=10000 -> palindrome 1000000001? length10, int=1000000001, okay. No leading zero. Good.

Potential issue: For n even, half_len=n//2. For n=10, start=10^4=10000, end=100000, half values 10000..99999. pal length 10. Good.

Potential issue: For n odd, half_len=(n+1)//2. For n=9, start=10^4=10000, end=100000, half values 10000..99999, pal length9? s length5, s[-2::-1] length4, total9. Good. For n=1, half_len=1, start=1, end=10, pal length1. Good.

Potential issue: In count_permutations, using den = product fact[c]. For cnt tuple from palindrome, sum n. fact[n]//den integer. Good.
bad_den = fact[cnt[0]-1] * product fact[1..9]. If cnt[0]=0 skip. If cnt[0]>0, cnt[0]-1 >=0. Good.
bad = fact[n-1] // bad_den. Is bad_den divides fact[n-1]? Yes because it's number of permutations of remaining multiset. Need if cnt[0]=0 skip. If cnt0>0, remaining length n-1, counts cnt0-1 and others. Product factorial divides (n-1)!. Good.
ans += total - bad. total-bad nonnegative. Good.

Potential issue: If cnt tuple contains all zeros? Not possible from palindrome with no leading zero. But if n=0 no. Good.

Potential issue: The set good might contain tuples with sum n? yes.

Let's consider if there is any need to sort or use dictionary? no.

Now, let's think about possible hidden issue: "integer x is called k-palindromic if x is a palindrome and x is divisible by k." It doesn't explicitly say x has n digits in the definition, but in context "Return count of good integers containing n digits" and "digits can be rearranged to form a k-palindromic integer" implies the rearranged integer has same digits, hence n digits. Good.

Now, let's think about if original integer can be rearranged to form a k-palindromic integer that is not necessarily using all digits? No, "its digits" all. Good.

Now, let's think about if count of good integers includes integers with leading zeros? No, "containing n digits" means no leading zeros. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a simpler combinatorial formula but not needed.

Let's write final code with class Solution and method.

Potential code style:

class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i

        good = set()
        half_len = (n + 1) // 2
        start = 10 ** (half_len - 1)
        end = 10 ** half_len

        for half in range(start, end):
            s = str(half)
            if n % 2 == 0:
                pal = s + s[::-1]
            else:
                if n == 1:
                    pal = s
                else:
                    pal = s + s[-2::-1]

            if int(pal) % k == 0:
                cnt = [0] * 10
                for ch in pal:
                    cnt[ord(ch) - 48] += 1
                good.add(tuple(cnt))

        ans = 0
        for cnt in good:
            den = 1
            for c in cnt:
                den *= fact[c]
            total = fact[n] // den

            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad_den = fact[cnt[0] - 1]
                for d in range(1, 10):
                    bad_den *= fact[cnt[d]]
                total -= fact[n - 1] // bad_den

            ans += total

        return ans

class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i

        good = set()
        half_len = (n + 1) // 2
        start = 10 ** (half_len - 1)
        end = 10 ** half_len

        for half in range(start, end):
            s = str(half)
            if n % 2 == 0:
                pal = s + s[::-1]
            else:
                if n == 1:
                    pal = s
                else:
                    pal = s + s[-2::-1]

            if int(pal) % k == 0:
                cnt = [0] * 10
                for ch in pal:
                    cnt[int(ch)] += 1
                good.add(tuple(cnt))

        ans = 0
        for cnt in good:
            den = 1
            for c in cnt:
                den *= fact[c]
            total = fact[n] // den

            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad_den = fact[cnt[0] - 1]
                for d in range(1, 10):
                    bad_den *= fact[cnt[d]]
                total -= fact[n - 1] // bad_den

            ans += total

        return ans

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i

        good = set()
        half_len = (n + 1) // 2
        start = 10 ** (half_len - 1)
        end = 10 ** half_len

        for half in range(start, end):
            s = str(half)

            if n % 2 == 0:
                pal = s + s[::-1]
            else:
                if n == 1:
                    pal = s
                else:
                    pal = s + s[-2::-1]

            if int(pal) % k == 0:
                cnt = [0] * 10
                for ch in pal:
                    cnt[int(ch)] += 1
                good.add(tuple(cnt))

        ans = 0

        for cnt in good:
            den = 1
            for c in cnt:
                den *= fact[c]

            total = fact[n] // den

            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad_den = fact[cnt[0] - 1]
                for d in range(1, 10):
                    bad_den *= fact[cnt[d]]
                total -= fact[n - 1] // bad_den

            ans += total

        return ans
```
